<a id='question-100'></a>

### Question 100: Medically Relevant Viruses: Part 2 Focus 50 ((release)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis of Herpes Simplex Virus (HSV) encephalitis, specifically the characteristic histopathological findings and the mechanism of neuronal damage caused by viral replication and the host immune response. (理解单纯疱疹病毒 (HSV) 脑炎的病理生理机制，特别是病毒复制和宿主免疫反应引起的神经元损伤的特征性组织病理学发现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old previously healthy male presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of fever, severe headache, confusion, and focal seizures involving his right arm. He denies recent travel, sick contacts, or intravenous drug use. His neurological examination reveals altered mental status (Glasgow Coma Scale 11), right-sided hemiparesis, and abnormal reflexes. A non-contrast head CT scan shows diffuse cerebral edema and low-density lesions in the temporal lobes. Lumbar puncture reveals cloudy cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) with elevated protein (150 mg/dL), low glucose (30 mg/dL), and 100 white blood cells/µL, predominantly lymphocytes. CSF PCR for HSV-1 is positive. Brain biopsy is performed. Histopathological examination of the brain tissue shows extensive necrosis, inflammation, and characteristic intranuclear inclusions within neurons and glial cells, particularly prominent in the temporal lobes. Multinucleated giant cells are also observed.

Which of the following pathological findings is MOST characteristic of HSV encephalitis?

(A) Formation of Cowdry type A intranuclear inclusions in keratinocytes of the epidermis.
(B) Presence of Negri bodies within Purkinje neurons of the cerebellum.
(C) Formation of syncytia due to fusion of infected cells, often seen in respiratory epithelium.
(D) Presence of glial nodules surrounding demyelinated axons in the white matter.
(E) Formation of characteristic intranuclear inclusions (Cowdry type B) and multinucleated giant cells within neurons and glial cells, particularly in the temporal lobes.

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male, previously healthy. This suggests an acute infection rather than a chronic condition or immunodeficiency-related opportunistic infection.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, severe headache, confusion, focal seizures (right arm), altered mental status (GCS 11), right hemiparesis. These point towards acute encephalitis, likely affecting the frontal and temporal lobes given the altered mental status and focal neurological deficits.
- **CSF Findings:** Cloudy CSF, elevated protein, low glucose, lymphocytic pleocytosis. This is typical for viral encephalitis, although bacterial meningitis can also present similarly (though often with higher WBC counts and predominantly neutrophils initially).
- **Imaging:** CT shows diffuse cerebral edema and low-density lesions in the temporal lobes. Temporal lobe involvement is highly suggestive of HSV encephalitis.
- **Diagnosis:** CSF PCR positive for HSV-1. This confirms the diagnosis of HSV encephalitis.
- **Histopathology:** Brain biopsy shows extensive necrosis, inflammation, intranuclear inclusions within neurons and glial cells (especially temporal lobes), and multinucleated giant cells. This is the key information for the question.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST characteristic pathological finding of HSV encephalitis based on the provided clinical and histopathological context.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **HSV Pathogenesis:** HSV-1 is a neurotropic virus. Primary infection is often asymptomatic or causes oral lesions (cold sores). Reactivation can lead to encephalitis, typically affecting the temporal and frontal lobes. The virus replicates within neurons and glial cells, causing direct cytopathic effects (cell lysis) and triggering an inflammatory response.
- **Histopathology of HSV Encephalitis:** The hallmark findings include:
    - **Necrosis:** Extensive neuronal and glial cell death, often hemorrhagic.
    - **Inflammation:** Perivascular lymphocytic infiltrate.
    - **Intranuclear Inclusions:** Eosinophilic inclusions within the nuclei of infected cells (neurons and glial cells). These are often described as Cowdry type B inclusions (large, dense, centrally located, often marginated by chromatin).
    - **Multinucleated Giant Cells:** Formed by the fusion of infected cells, containing multiple nuclei, some with inclusions.
    - **Temporal Lobe Predilection:** HSV encephalitis classically affects the medial temporal lobes, insula, and orbitofrontal cortex.
- **Cowdry Inclusions:** These are intranuclear inclusions seen in cells infected with certain viruses.
    - **Cowdry type A:** Found in herpesvirus infections (HSV, VZV, CMV), adenovirus, and poxviruses. They are typically smaller, more basophilic, and peripherally marginated by chromatin. Often seen in keratinocytes (e.g., VZV in chickenpox).
    - **Cowdry type B:** Found primarily in poliovirus and HSV infections. They are larger, more eosinophilic, centrally located, and often surrounded by a clear halo. Seen in neurons and glial cells.
- **Other Viral Pathologies:**
    - **Rabies:** Causes encephalitis; characteristic finding is Negri bodies (eosinophilic cytoplasmic inclusions) in Purkinje neurons of the cerebellum and pyramidal neurons of the hippocampus.
    - **Respiratory Syncytial Virus (RSV) / Parainfluenza Virus:** Can cause bronchiolitis and pneumonia; characteristic finding is syncytia formation (fusion of infected cells) in respiratory epithelium.
    - **Subacute Sclerosing Panencephalitis (SSPE):** A rare, late complication of measles virus infection; characterized by glial nodules (aggregates of activated microglia and astrocytes) surrounding demyelinated axons in the white matter.

##### 3. Linking Vignette to Correct Answer:
The vignette describes a classic case of HSV encephalitis confirmed by PCR. The histopathology described (necrosis, inflammation, intranuclear inclusions in neurons/glia, multinucleated giant cells, temporal lobe involvement) perfectly matches the characteristic features of HSV encephalitis. Option (E) accurately describes these key pathological findings, specifically mentioning the intranuclear inclusions (Cowdry type B) and multinucleated giant cells within neurons and glial cells, particularly in the temporal lobes.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Formation of Cowdry type A intranuclear inclusions in keratinocytes of the epidermis. This describes a finding typically associated with Varicella-Zoster Virus (VZV) infection (chickenpox or shingles), not HSV encephalitis. While HSV can cause skin lesions, the characteristic histopathology of HSV encephalitis occurs in the brain, and the inclusions are typically Cowdry type B in neurons/glia, not type A in keratinocytes.
- (B): Presence of Negri bodies within Purkinje neurons of the cerebellum. Negri bodies are pathognomonic for Rabies virus infection, which causes a different type of encephalitis, often affecting the brainstem and cerebellum, and presenting with hydrophobia and aerophobia.
- (C): Formation of syncytia due to fusion of infected cells, often seen in respiratory epithelium. Syncytia formation is characteristic of viruses like Respiratory Syncytial Virus (RSV), Parainfluenza virus, and Measles virus, which primarily infect respiratory or other epithelial cells, not typically the brain in this manner (though measles can cause SSPE, which has different pathology).
- (D): Presence of glial nodules surrounding demyelinated axons in the white matter. This describes the characteristic pathology of Subacute Sclerosing Panencephalitis (SSPE), a rare, progressive neurological disorder caused by a persistent infection of the central nervous system with a defective measles virus.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | HSV Encephalitis (Correct Answer) | VZV Infection (Distractor A) | Rabies (Distractor B) | RSV/Parainfluenza (Distractor C) | SSPE (Distractor D) |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :----------------- |
| **Primary Site**         | Brain (Temporal Lobe)             | Skin (Epidermis)            | Brain (Brainstem/Cerebellum) | Respiratory Epithelium          | Brain (White Matter) |
| **Characteristic Inclusion** | Cowdry Type B (Intranuclear)      | Cowdry Type A (Intranuclear) | Negri Body (Cytoplasmic) | None (Syncytia)                  | None (Glial Nodules) |
| **Characteristic Cell** | Neurons, Glial Cells              | Keratinocytes               | Purkinje Neurons     | Epithelial Cells                  | Glial Cells        |
| **Other Features**      | Multinucleated Giant Cells, Necrosis | Vesicles                   | Hydrophobia          | Syncytia Formation                | Demyelination      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
HSV encephalitis is a medical emergency characterized by temporal lobe involvement, fever, altered mental status, and seizures. The definitive diagnosis often relies on CSF PCR for HSV DNA. Histopathology reveals characteristic intranuclear inclusions (Cowdry type B) and multinucleated giant cells in neurons and glial cells. Differentiating HSV encephalitis from other causes of encephalitis (like VZV, rabies, SSPE) requires recognizing their distinct clinical presentations and pathological hallmarks.
